| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1969-04` | \[N'(t)=1000e^{-t/20}\left(1-\frac{t}{20}\right).\]
The only interior critical time is \(t=20\). Compare
\[N(0)=25000,\quad N(20)=25000+\frac{20000}{e},\quad N(100)=25000+\frac{100000}{e^5}.\]
Thus the largest number is \(25000+\dfrac{20000}{e}\) at \(t=20\), and the smallest number is \(25000\) at \(t=0\).; We minimize \(N'(t)\). Its derivative is
\[N''(t)=\frac{1000}{400}e^{-t/20}(t-40).\]
Therefore \(N'\) decreases until \(t=40\) and increases afterward. The minimum rate occurs at \(t=40\), and
\[N'(40)=-\frac{1000}{e^2}\ \text{bacteria per unit time}.\] | \[N'(t)=1000e^{-t/20}\left(1-\frac{t}{20}\right).\]
The only interior critical time is \(t=20\). Compare
\[N(0)=25000,\quad N(20)=25000+\frac{20000}{e},\quad N(100)=25000+\frac{100000}{e^5}.\]
Thus the largest number is \(25000+\dfrac{20000}{e}\) at \(t=20\), and the smallest number is \(25000\) at \(t=0\).; We minimize \(N'(t)\). Its derivative is
\[N''(t)=\frac{1000}{400}e^{-t/20}(t-40).\]
Therefore \(N'\) decreases until \(t=40\) and increases afterward. The minimum rate occurs at \(t=40\), and
\[N'(40)=-\frac{1000}{e^2}\ \text{bacteria per unit time}.\] | verified | true |

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1969-06` | By symmetry write \(y=h+Ax^2\). Using \(y(b)=0\) gives \(A=-h/b^2\). Thus
\[y=h\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right),\qquad -b\le x\le b.\]; The cosine should have maximum \(h\) at \(x=0\) and zeros at \(x=\pm b\). Therefore
\[y=h\cos\left(\frac{\pi x}{2b}\right),\qquad -b\le x\le b.\]; \[A_P=\int_{-b}^{b}h\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right)dx=\frac{4bh}{3},\]
\[A_C=\int_{-b}^{b}h\cos\left(\frac{\pi x}{2b}\right)dx=\frac{4bh}{\pi}.\]
Since \(\pi>3\), \(\frac1{3}>\frac1\pi\), so the parabolic window has greater area. | By symmetry write \(y=h+Ax^2\). Using \(y(b)=0\) gives \(A=-h/b^2\). Thus
\[y=h\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right),\qquad -b\le x\le b.\]; The cosine should have maximum \(h\) at \(x=0\) and zeros at \(x=\pm b\). Therefore
\[y=h\cos\left(\frac{\pi x}{2b}\right),\qquad -b\le x\le b.\]; \[A_P=\int_{-b}^{b}h\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right)dx=\frac{4bh}{3},\]
\[A_C=\int_{-b}^{b}h\cos\left(\frac{\pi x}{2b}\right)dx=\frac{4bh}{\pi}.\]
Since \(\pi>3\), \(\frac1{3}>\frac1\pi\), so the parabolic window has greater area. | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1969-07` | The graph is even, passes through \((0,1)\), has a horizontal tangent and absolute minimum at \((0,1)\), is concave up everywhere, and grows without bound as \(x\to\pm\infty\).; At \(x=r\),

\[\lim_{r\to\infty}PQ=\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1+e^{-2r}}{1-e^{-2r}}=1.\] | The graph is even, passes through \((0,1)\), has a horizontal tangent and absolute minimum at \((0,1)\), is concave up everywhere, and grows without bound as \(x\to\pm\infty\).; At \(x=r\),

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1970-01` | The derivative is

\qquad\boxed{y=4-\frac{x}{2}}.
\]; Solve the first-quadrant intersection:
\[
x^2-2x+3=4-\frac{x}{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2x^2-3x-2=0,
\]
so \(x=2\) or \(x=-\tfrac12\). The relevant intersection is \(x=2\). The line meets the \(x\)-axis at \(x=8\). On \([0,2]\), the parabola is lower; on \([2,8]\), the line is lower. Thus
\[
A=\int_0^2(x^2-2x+3)\,dx+\int_2^8\left(4-\frac{x}{2}\right)\,dx.
\]
The two contributions are \(\tfrac{14}{3}\) and \(9\), so
\[
\boxed{A=\frac{41}{3}}.
\] | The derivative is

\[

\qquad\boxed{y=4-\frac{x}{2}}.
\]; Solve the first-quadrant intersection:
\[

\[

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1970-03` | For \(x\ne0\),
\[
f'(x)=\frac43x^{1/3}+\frac43x^{-2/3}=\frac{4(x+1)}{3x^{2/3}}.
\]
A horizontal tangent requires \(f'(x)=0\), so \(x=-1\). Since \(f(-1)=1-4=-3\), the point is
\[
\boxed{(-1,-3)}.
\]; At \(x=0\), the function is continuous and
\[
f'(x)=\frac{4(x+1)}{3x^{2/3}}\longrightarrow+\infty
\]
from both sides. Therefore the graph has a vertical tangent at
\[
\boxed{(0,0)}.
\]; Check the endpoints, the critical point \(x=-1\), and the nondifferentiable interior point \(x=0\):
\[
f(-8)=8,\quad f(-1)=-3,\quad f(0)=0,\quad f(8)=24.
\]
Hence the absolute minimum is \(\boxed{(-1,-3)}\), and the absolute maximum is \(\boxed{(8,24)}\).; For \(x\ne0\),
\[
f''(x)=\frac{4(x-2)}{9x^{5/3}}.
\]
This is negative exactly when \(0<x<2\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{\text{concave down on }(0,2)}.
\]; The graph decreases from \((-8,8)\) to the horizontal-tangent absolute minimum \((-1,-3)\), then increases through the vertical tangent \((0,0)\) and continues to the absolute maximum \((8,24)\). It is concave up on \((-8,0)\), concave down on \((0,2)\), and concave up on \((2,8)\). \]

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1970-06` | A parabola symmetric about the \(y\)-axis with vertex at the origin has form \(y=ax^2\). Substituting \((b,e^{-b^2})\) gives
\[
a=\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2},
\]
so
\[
\boxed{y=\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2}x^2}.
\]; Using cylindrical shells, a vertical shell has radius \(x\) and height
\[
e^{-b^2}-\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2}x^2=e^{-b^2}\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right).
\]
Thus
\[
V=2\pi e^{-b^2}\int_0^b x\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right)dx
=\boxed{\frac{\pi}{2}b^2e^{-b^2}}.
\]
A washer integral with respect to \(y\) gives the same result.; Differentiate:
\[
V'(b)=\pi b e^{-b^2}(1-b^2).
\]
For \(b>0\), the only critical value is \(b=1\). The derivative is positive for \(0<b<1\) and negative for \(b>1\), so the volume is maximized at
\[
\boxed{b=1}.
\]
The maximum volume is \(\pi/(2e)\). | A parabola symmetric about the \(y\)-axis with vertex at the origin has form \(y=ax^2\). Substituting \((b,e^{-b^2})\) gives
\[
a=\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2},
\]
so
\[
\boxed{y=\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2}x^2}.
\]; Using cylindrical shells, a vertical shell has radius \(x\) and height
\[
e^{-b^2}-\frac{e^{-b^2}}{b^2}x^2=e^{-b^2}\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right).
\]
Thus
\[
V=2\pi e^{-b^2}\int_0^b x\left(1-\frac{x^2}{b^2}\right)dx
=\boxed{\frac{\pi}{2}b^2e^{-b^2}}.
\]
A washer integral with respect to \(y\) gives the same result.; Differentiate:
\[
V'(b)=\pi b e^{-b^2}(1-b^2).
\]
For \(b>0\), the only critical value is \(b=1\). The derivative is positive for \(0<b<1\) and negative for \(b>1\), so the volume is maximized at
\[
\boxed{b=1}.
\]
The maximum volume is \(\pi/(2e)\). | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1970-07` | Integrate the given inequality from \(0\) to \(x\):
\[
\int_0^x\sin t\,dt\le\int_0^x t\,dt.
\]
Because \(x\ge0\), the order is preserved. Therefore
\[
1-\cos x\le\frac{x^2}{2},
\]
which rearranges to
\[
\boxed{1-\frac{x^2}{2}\le\cos x}.
\]; From part (a), for every \(u\ge0\),
\[
\cos u\ge1-\frac{u^2}{2}.
\]
Integrate from \(0\) to \(t\):
\[
\sin t=\int_0^t\cos u\,du\ge t-\frac{t^3}{6}.
\]
Now integrate from \(0\) to \(x\):
\[
1-\cos x=\int_0^x\sin t\,dt
\ge\frac{x^2}{2}-\frac{x^4}{24}.
\]
Rearranging gives
\[
\boxed{\cos x\le1-\frac{x^2}{2}+\frac{x^4}{24}}
=\boxed{1-\frac{x^2}{2!}+\frac{x^4}{4!}}.
\] | Integrate the given inequality from \(0\) to \(x\):

\boxed{\cos x\le1-\frac{x^2}{2}+\frac{x^4}{24}}

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-01` | Since

we require \(x^2-4>0\). Thus \(x^2>4\), so

\]; On its domain, \(x^2-4\) assumes every positive value. The natural logarithm maps \((0,\infty)\) onto all real numbers. Therefore
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Range}(H)=(-\infty,\infty)}.
\]; Because
\[

\]

\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Dom}(K)=(0,\infty)}.

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-02` | The curves meet where
\[
3x^2=\frac3x
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x^3=1,
\]
so the first-quadrant intersection is \(x=1\). On \([0,1]\), the lower curve is \(3x^2\); on \([1,k]\), it is \(3/x\). Hence
\[
A(k)=\int_0^1 3x^2\,dx+\int_1^k\frac3x\,dx
=1+3\ln k.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{A(k)=1+3\ln k},\qquad k>1.
\]; Set the area equal to \(7\):
\[
1+3\ln k=7
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\ln k=2.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{k=e^2}.
\]; Differentiate \(A=1+3\ln k\) with respect to time:
\[
\frac{dA}{dt}=\frac3k\frac{dk}{dt}.
\]
At \(k=15\) and \(dA/dt=5\),
\[
5=\frac3{15}\frac{dk}{dt}.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\frac{dk}{dt}=25\text{ units per second}}.
\] | The curves meet where

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-03` | Factor:
\[
F(x)=\cos x(\cos x+2).
\]
Since \(\cos x=-2\) is impossible, we need \(\cos x=0\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{x=\frac\pi2,\;\frac{3\pi}{2}}.
\]; Complete the square:
\[
F(x)=(\cos x+1)^2-1.
\]
Because a square is nonnegative, the smallest possible value is \(-1\), attained when \(\cos x=-1\). In the stated period this occurs at
\[
\boxed{x=\pi},
\qquad F(\pi)=-1.
\]
This is the absolute minimum on the period.; Differentiate twice:
\[
F'(x)=-2\sin x(\cos x+1),
\]
\[
F''(x)=-2(2\cos^2x+\cos x-1)
=-2(2\cos x-1)(\cos x+1).
\]
Since \(\cos x+1\ge0\), the second derivative is positive when \(\cos x<\tfrac12\), except that it equals zero at the isolated point \(x=\pi\). Thus the graph is concave up throughout
\[
\boxed{\left(\frac\pi3,\frac{5\pi}{3}\right)}.
\]
The isolated zero of \(F''\) at \(x=\pi\) does not produce a change in concavity. | Factor:
\[
F(x)=\cos x(\cos x+2).
\]
Since \(\cos x=-2\) is impossible, we need \(\cos x=0\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{x=\frac\pi2,\;\frac{3\pi}{2}}.
\]; Complete the square:
\[
F(x)=(\cos x+1)^2-1.
\]
Because a square is nonnegative, the smallest possible value is \(-1\), attained when \(\cos x=-1\). In the stated period this occurs at
\[
\boxed{x=\pi},
\qquad F(\pi)=-1.
\]
This is the absolute minimum on the period.; Differentiate twice:
\[
F'(x)=-2\sin x(\cos x+1),
\]
\[
F''(x)=-2(2\cos^2x+\cos x-1)
=-2(2\cos x-1)(\cos x+1).
\]
Since \(\cos x+1\ge0\), the second derivative is positive when \(\cos x<\tfrac12\), except that it equals zero at the isolated point \(x=\pi\). Thus the graph is concave up throughout
\[
\boxed{\left(\frac\pi3,\frac{5\pi}{3}\right)}.
\]
The isolated zero of \(F''\) at \(x=\pi\) does not produce a change in concavity. | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-04` | The curves intersect where
\[
18-x^2=2x^2-9,
\]
so \(x=\pm3\). By symmetry, let the vertical sides of the rectangle be \(x=\pm a\), where \(0\le a\le3\). Its width is \(2a\), and its height is
\[
(18-a^2)-(2a^2-9)=27-3a^2.
\]
Therefore
\[
A(a)=2a(27-3a^2)=54a-6a^3.
\]
Differentiate:
\[
A'(a)=54-18a^2=18(3-a^2).
\]
The positive critical value is \(a=\sqrt3\). Also,
\[
A''(a)=-36a<0
\]
at \(a=\sqrt3\), so this critical point gives a maximum. The dimensions are
\[
\text{width}=2\sqrt3,
\qquad
\text{height}=27-3(3)=18.
\]
Hence the largest area is
\[
\boxed{A_{\max}=36\sqrt3}.
\] | The curves intersect where

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-05` | The curve meets the \(x\)-axis at \(x=0\) and \(x=2\). Using disks,
\[

\]

\[
(2x-x^2)^2=4x^2-4x^3+x^4,
\]
\[
V_x=\pi\left[\frac{4x^3}{3}-x^4+\frac{x^5}{5}\right]_0^2
=\boxed{\frac{16\pi}{15}}.
\]; A washer solution remains within current AP Calculus AB methods. Write
\[
y=1-(x-1)^2,
\]
so for \(0\le y\le1\), the outer and inner radii are
\[
R(y)=1+\sqrt{1-y},
\qquad r(y)=1-\sqrt{1-y}.
\]
Therefore
\[
V_y=\pi\int_0^1\left[(1+\sqrt{1-y})^2-(1-\sqrt{1-y})^2\right]dy.
\]
The bracket simplifies to \(4\sqrt{1-y}\), so
\[
V_y=4\pi\int_0^1\sqrt{1-y}\,dy
=\boxed{\frac{8\pi}{3}}.
\]
Equivalently, cylindrical shells give \(2\pi\int_0^2x(2x-x^2)\,dx\). | The curve meets the \(x\)-axis at \(x=0\) and \(x=2\). Using disks,

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-06` | Differentiate:
\[
v'(t)=\frac{1-t^2}{(1+t^2)^2}.
\]
For \(t>0\), this derivative is positive on \((0,1)\) and negative on \((1,\infty)\). Thus the absolute maximum occurs at \(t=1\), with
\[
\boxed{v_{\max}=v(1)=\frac12}.

\[
x(t)=5+\int_0^t\frac{s}{1+s^2}\,ds
=5+\frac12\ln(1+t^2).
\]

\[
\boxed{x(6)=5+\frac12\ln37}.
\]; Divide numerator and denominator by \(t^2\):
\[
v(t)=\frac{1/t}{1/t^2+1}\longrightarrow0.
\]

\[
\boxed{\lim_{t\to\infty}v(t)=0}.
\]; Although the velocity tends to zero, the position
\[
x(t)=5+\frac12\ln(1+t^2)
\]
is unbounded because \(\ln(1+t^2)\to\infty\). Therefore the particle eventually passes every point to the right, including \(x=500\). In fact, the crossing time satisfies
\[

\]
so
\[
t=\sqrt{e^{990}-1},
\]
a finite but extremely large time. Thus \(
\boxed{\text{yes}}
\). | Differentiate:

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1971-07` | Because \(&#124;-x&#124;=&#124;x&#124;\) and \((-x)^2=x^2\),
\[
f(-x)=&#124;-x&#124;^{1/2}e^{-(-x)^2}=&#124;x&#124;^{1/2}e^{-x^2}=f(x).
\]
Therefore \(f\) is even and its graph is symmetric about the \(y\)-axis:
\[
\boxed{f(-x)=f(x)}.
\]; For \(x>0\),
\[
f(x)=x^{1/2}e^{-x^2},
\]
so
\[
f'(x)=e^{-x^2}\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}-2x^{3/2}\right)
=\frac{e^{-x^2}(1-4x^2)}{2\sqrt{x}}.
\]
Thus \(f'(x)>0\) for \(0<x<\tfrac12\) and \(f'(x)<0\) for \(x>\tfrac12\). Since \(f\) is even, its derivative behavior reflects across the \(y\)-axis. Therefore \(f\) is increasing on
\[
\boxed{(-\infty,-\tfrac12)\cup(0,\tfrac12)}.
\]; The function is nonnegative and even. It satisfies \(f(0)=0\), but the one-sided slopes become unbounded in magnitude, producing a sharp minimum at the origin. The critical points away from the origin are \(x=\pm\tfrac12\). At both points,
\[
f\left(\pm\frac12\right)
=\sqrt{\frac12}e^{-1/4}
=\frac{e^{-1/4}}{\sqrt2}.
\]
They are equal absolute maxima. Finally,
\[
\lim_{&#124;x&#124;\to\infty}&#124;x&#124;^{1/2}e^{-x^2}=0,
\]
so the graph approaches the \(x\)-axis from above on both ends. The feature list is therefore
\[
\boxed{\text{minimum }(0,0),\quad
\text{maxima }\left(\pm\frac12,\frac{e^{-1/4}}{\sqrt2}\right),\quad
f(x)\to0.}
\] | Because \(&#124;-x&#124;=&#124;x&#124;\) and \((-x)^2=x^2\),

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1972-01` | Factor the polynomial:
\[
4x^3-3x-1=(x-1)(4x^2+4x+1)=(x-1)(2x+1)^2.

\[
\boxed{(1,0)\text{ and }\left(-\frac12,0\right)}.
\]
The intercept at \(x=-\tfrac12\) has even multiplicity, so the graph touches the axis there.; Differentiate:
\[
f'(x)=12x^2-3.

\[

\[
y-25=45(x-2),
\]
so
\[
\boxed{y=45x-65}.
\]; Reflection across the \(y\)-axis replaces \(x\) by \(-x\):
\[
y=f(-x)=4(-x)^3-3(-x)-1.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{y=-4x^3+3x-1}.
\] | Factor the polynomial:

\[

\boxed{t=2\text{ and }t=5}.

\[
\boxed{t=5}.

\]

\[
\boxed{x=-27},
\]
which is \(27\) units to the left of the origin. | Differentiate the position:

The triangle has base \(\pi/k\) and height \(\pi k/2\). Thus
\[
A_\triangle=\frac12\left(\frac{\pi}{k}\right)\left(\frac{\pi k}{2}\right)
=\boxed{\frac{\pi^2}{4}}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1972-04` | The rectangle has dimensions \(x\) by \(2x\), so its perimeter is \(6x\). Let the square side be \(s\). Using all the fencing,
\[
6x+4s=340,
\qquad s=85-\frac32x.
\]
The rectangle-area constraint gives
\[
2x^2\ge800\quad\Rightarrow\quad x\ge20.
\]
The square-area constraint gives \(s^2\ge100\). Since \(s>0\), \(s\ge10\), so
\[
85-\frac32x\ge10\quad\Rightarrow\quad x\le50.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{20\le x\le50}.
\]; The total area is
\[
A(x)=2x^2+s^2=2x^2+\left(85-\frac32x\right)^2
=\frac{17}{4}x^2-255x+7225.
\]
Because \(A''(x)=\tfrac{17}{2}>0\), this quadratic is concave up, so its maximum on the closed interval \([20,50]\) occurs at an endpoint.
\[
A(20)=800+55^2=3825,
\]
\[
A(50)=5000+10^2=5100.
\]
Therefore the greatest possible total area is
\[
\boxed{5100\text{ square yards}},
\]
achieved with a \(50\times100\) rectangle and a \(10\times10\) square. | The rectangle has dimensions \(x\) by \(2x\), so its perimeter is \(6x\). Let the square side be \(s\). Using all the fencing,

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1972-05` | By the chain rule,
\[
\frac{dy}{dx}=2e^{\cos x}(-\sin x)
=\boxed{-2e^{\cos x}\sin x}.
\]
Differentiate again:
\[
\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}
=-2\left[e^{\cos x}(-\sin x)\sin x+e^{\cos x}\cos x\right].
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}=2e^{\cos x}(\sin^2x-\cos x)}.
\]; Differentiate with respect to time:
\[
\frac{dy}{dt}=\frac{dy}{dx}\frac{dx}{dt}
=-2e^{\cos x}\sin x\frac{dx}{dt}.
\]
At \(x=\pi/2\), \(e^{\cos x}=1\) and \(\sin x=1\). Since \(dy/dt=5\),
\[
5=-2\frac{dx}{dt}.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\frac{dx}{dt}=-\frac52\text{ units per second}}.
\] | By the chain rule,
\[
\frac{dy}{dx}=2e^{\cos x}(-\sin x)
=\boxed{-2e^{\cos x}\sin x}.
\]

\[

=-2\left[e^{\cos x}(-\sin x)\sin x+e^{\cos x}\cos x\right].
\]
Therefore
\[

\]; Differentiate with respect to time:
\[

=-2e^{\cos x}\sin x\frac{dx}{dt}.
\]
At \(x=\pi/2\), \(e^{\cos x}=1\) and \(\sin x=1\). Since \(dy/dt=5\),
\[
5=-2\frac{dx}{dt}.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\frac{dx}{dt}=-\frac52\text{ units per second}}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1972-06` | Using disks of radius \(1/x\),
\[
V=\pi\int_1^2\left(\frac1x\right)^2dx
=\pi\int_1^2x^{-2}\,dx.
\]
Thus
\[
V=\pi\left[-\frac1x\right]_1^2
=\pi\left(1-\frac12\right)
=\boxed{\frac\pi2}.
\]; Using cylindrical shells, the radius is \(3-x\) and the height is \(1/x\):
\[
V=2\pi\int_1^2(3-x)\frac1x\,dx
=2\pi\int_1^2\left(\frac3x-1\right)dx.
\]
Therefore
\[
V=2\pi[3\ln x-x]_1^2
=2\pi(3\ln2-1).
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{V=2\pi(3\ln2-1)}.
\]
A current-AB washer solution is also possible by integrating horizontally in two pieces. | Using disks of radius \(1/x\),
\[
V=\pi\int_1^2\left(\frac1x\right)^2dx
=\pi\int_1^2x^{-2}\,dx.

\[

=\pi\left(1-\frac12\right)
=\boxed{\frac\pi2}.
\]; Using cylindrical shells, the radius is \(3-x\) and the height is \(1/x\):
\[
V=2\pi\int_1^2(3-x)\frac1x\,dx
=2\pi\int_1^2\left(\frac3x-1\right)dx.
\]
Therefore
\[
V=2\pi[3\ln x-x]_1^2
=2\pi(3\ln2-1).
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{V=2\pi(3\ln2-1)}.
\]
A current-AB washer solution is also possible by integrating horizontally in two pieces. | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1972-07` | Substitute \(a=1\) and \(b=2\):
\[
f(3)-f(1)=2k+8.
\]
Since \(21-5=16\),
\[
16=2k+8,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{k=4}.
\]; Use the derivative definition with increment \(b\):
\[
f'(a)=\lim_{b\to0}\frac{f(a+b)-f(a)}{b}
=\lim_{b\to0}(ka+2b)=ka.
\]
With \(k=4\),
\[
\boxed{f'(3)=12}.
\]; From the derivative calculation,
\[
\boxed{f'(x)=4x}.
\]
Therefore
\[
f(x)=2x^2+C.
\]
Using \(f(1)=5\),
\[
2+C=5\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=3.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{f(x)=2x^2+3}.
\]
Indeed,
\[
f(a+b)-f(a)=2(a+b)^2-2a^2=4ab+2b^2,
\]
so the function satisfies the original identity. | Substitute \(a=1\) and \(b=2\):
\[
f(3)-f(1)=2k+8.
\]
Since \(21-5=16\),
\[
16=2k+8,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{k=4}.
\]; Use the derivative definition with increment \(b\):
\[
f'(a)=\lim_{b\to0}\frac{f(a+b)-f(a)}{b}
=\lim_{b\to0}(ka+2b)=ka.
\]
With \(k=4\),
\[
\boxed{f'(3)=12}.
\]; From the derivative calculation,
\[
\boxed{f'(x)=4x}.
\]
Therefore
\[
f(x)=2x^2+C.
\]
Using \(f(1)=5\),
\[
2+C=5\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=3.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{f(x)=2x^2+3}.
\]
Indeed,
\[
f(a+b)-f(a)=2(a+b)^2-2a^2=4ab+2b^2,
\]
so the function satisfies the original identity. | verified | true |

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-01` | Set \(f(x)=4\):
\[
x^3-6x^2+9x-4=0.
\]
The polynomial factors as
\[
(x-1)^2(x-4)=0.
\]
Thus \(x=1\) or \(x=4\), and the common points are
\[
\boxed{(1,4)\text{ and }(4,4)}.
\]; Factor:
\[
f(x)=x(x^2-6x+9)=x(x-3)^2.
\]
Therefore the zeros are
\[
\boxed{x=0\text{ and }x=3}.
\]
The zero at \(x=3\) has multiplicity two.; Differentiate:
\[
f'(x)=3x^2-12x+9=3(x-1)(x-3).
\]
On \([0,2]\), the only interior critical point is \(x=1\). Evaluate the endpoints and this critical point:
\[
f(0)=0,\qquad f(1)=4,\qquad f(2)=2.
\]
The function is continuous, so it attains every value between its absolute minimum and maximum. Hence the range is
\[
\boxed{[0,4]}.
\] | Set \(f(x)=4\):
\[
x^3-6x^2+9x-4=0.
\]
The polynomial factors as
\[
(x-1)^2(x-4)=0.
\]
Thus \(x=1\) or \(x=4\), and the common points are
\[
\boxed{(1,4)\text{ and }(4,4)}.
\]; Factor:
\[
f(x)=x(x^2-6x+9)=x(x-3)^2.
\]
Therefore the zeros are
\[
\boxed{x=0\text{ and }x=3}.
\]
The zero at \(x=3\) has multiplicity two.; Differentiate:
\[
f'(x)=3x^2-12x+9=3(x-1)(x-3).
\]
On \([0,2]\), the only interior critical point is \(x=1\). Evaluate the endpoints and this critical point:
\[
f(0)=0,\qquad f(1)=4,\qquad f(2)=2.
\]
The function is continuous, so it attains every value between its absolute minimum and maximum. Hence the range is
\[
\boxed{[0,4]}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-02` | Integrate the acceleration:
\[
v(t)=\int\left(\frac{t}{8}-\frac1{t^2}\right)dt=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t+C.
\]
Use \(v(1)=\frac9{16}\):
\[
\frac1{16}+1+C=\frac9{16}\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=-\frac12.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{v(t)=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t-\frac12},\qquad t>0.
\]; Yes. As \(t\to\infty\),
\[
v(t)=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t-\frac12\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Thus the velocity eventually exceeds any fixed positive number, including \(500\). For example,
\[
v(100)=625+0.01-0.5=624.51>500.
\]
Also, \(v(t)\to\infty\) as \(t\to0^+\) because of the \(1/t\) term.; Integrate the velocity:
\[
s(t)=\frac{t^3}{48}+\ln t-\frac t2+C.
\]
Use \(s(1)=\frac{25}{48}\):
\[
\frac1{48}-\frac12+C=\frac{25}{48}\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=1.
\]
Therefore
\[
s(2)=\frac8{48}+\ln2-1+1=\frac16+\ln2.
\]
This value is positive, so the distance from the origin is
\[
\boxed{\frac16+\ln2}.
\] | Integrate the acceleration:
\[
v(t)=\int\left(\frac{t}{8}-\frac1{t^2}\right)dt=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t+C.
\]

\[
\frac1{16}+1+C=\frac9{16}\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=-\frac12.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{v(t)=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t-\frac12},\qquad t>0.
\]; Yes. As \(t\to\infty\),
\[
v(t)=\frac{t^2}{16}+\frac1t-\frac12\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Thus the velocity eventually exceeds any fixed positive number, including \(500\). For example,
\[
v(100)=625+0.01-0.5=624.51>500.
\]
Also, \(v(t)\to\infty\) as \(t\to0^+\) because of the \(1/t\) term.; Integrate the velocity:

\[
\frac1{48}-\frac12+C=\frac{25}{48}\quad\Rightarrow\quad C=1.

\[
\boxed{\frac16+\ln2}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-03` | Differentiate implicitly with respect to \(x\):
\[
1+y+x\frac{dy}{dx}+4y\frac{dy}{dx}=0.
\]
Collect the derivative terms:
\[
(x+4y)\frac{dy}{dx}=-(1+y).
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{1+y}{x+4y}}.
\]; At \((2,1)\),
\[
\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{1+1}{2+4}=-\frac13.
\]
Therefore the tangent line is
\[
y-1=-\frac13(x-2),
\]
or equivalently
\[
\boxed{x+3y=5}.
\]; Set the slope equal to \(-1/3\):
\[
-\frac{1+y}{x+4y}=-\frac13\quad\Rightarrow\quad x=3-y.
\]
Substitute into the curve:
\[
(3-y)+(3-y)y+2y^2=6,
\]
so
\[
y^2+2y-3=0=(y-1)(y+3).
\]
The solution \(y=1\) gives the original point. The other solution is \(y=-3\), giving \(x=6\). Hence the other point is
\[
\boxed{(6,-3)}.
\] | Differentiate implicitly with respect to \(x\):
\[
1+y+x\frac{dy}{dx}+4y\frac{dy}{dx}=0.
\]
Collect the derivative terms:
\[
(x+4y)\frac{dy}{dx}=-(1+y).
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{1+y}{x+4y}}.
\]; At \((2,1)\),
\[
\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{1+1}{2+4}=-\frac13.
\]
Therefore the tangent line is
\[
y-1=-\frac13(x-2),
\]
or equivalently
\[
\boxed{x+3y=5}.
\]; Set the slope equal to \(-1/3\):
\[
-\frac{1+y}{x+4y}=-\frac13\quad\Rightarrow\quad x=3-y.
\]
Substitute into the curve:
\[
(3-y)+(3-y)y+2y^2=6,
\]
so
\[
y^2+2y-3=0=(y-1)(y+3).
\]
The solution \(y=1\) gives the original point. The other solution is \(y=-3\), giving \(x=6\). Hence the other point is
\[
\boxed{(6,-3)}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-04` | Using \(x=y^2/4\) in the line gives
\[
y=\frac{y^2}{2}+b,
\]
so
\[
y^2-2y+2b=0.
\]
There are two distinct intersections exactly when the discriminant is positive:
\[
(-2)^2-4(1)(2b)>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad 4-8b>0.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{b<\frac12}.
\]; Write each curve as \(x\) in terms of \(y\):
\[
x=\frac{y+4}{2},\qquad x=\frac{y^2}{4}.
\]
Their intersection values satisfy \(y^2-2y-8=0\), so \(y=-2,4\). The line is the right boundary, hence
\[
A=\int_{-2}^{4}\left(\frac{y+4}{2}-\frac{y^2}{4}\right)dy=\boxed{9}.
\]; For \(b=0\), the curves are \(y=2x\) and \(y=2\sqrt{x}\) in the bounded first-quadrant region. They meet at \(x=0\) and \(x=1\). Washers about the \(x\)-axis have outer radius \(2\sqrt{x}\) and inner radius \(2x\):
\[
V=\pi\int_0^1\left[(2\sqrt{x})^2-(2x)^2\right]dx
=4\pi\int_0^1(x-x^2)dx
=\boxed{\frac{2\pi}{3}}.
\] | Using \(x=y^2/4\) in the line gives
\[
y=\frac{y^2}{2}+b,
\]
so
\[
y^2-2y+2b=0.
\]
There are two distinct intersections exactly when the discriminant is positive:
\[
(-2)^2-4(1)(2b)>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad 4-8b>0.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{b<\frac12}.
\]; Write each curve as \(x\) in terms of \(y\):
\[
x=\frac{y+4}{2},\qquad x=\frac{y^2}{4}.
\]
Their intersection values satisfy \(y^2-2y-8=0\), so \(y=-2,4\). The line is the right boundary, hence
\[
A=\int_{-2}^{4}\left(\frac{y+4}{2}-\frac{y^2}{4}\right)dy=\boxed{9}.
\]; For \(b=0\), the curves are \(y=2x\) and \(y=2\sqrt{x}\) in the bounded first-quadrant region. They meet at \(x=0\) and \(x=1\). Washers about the \(x\)-axis have outer radius \(2\sqrt{x}\) and inner radius \(2x\):
\[
V=\pi\int_0^1\left[(2\sqrt{x})^2-(2x)^2\right]dx
=4\pi\int_0^1(x-x^2)dx
=\boxed{\frac{2\pi}{3}}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-05` | Differentiate:
\[
y'=e^{-kx}(1-kx).
\]
Because \(e^{-kx}>0\), the derivative is positive when \(x<1/k\) and negative when \(x>1/k\). Thus the function increases and then decreases, so its absolute maximum occurs at \(x=1/k\). The maximum value is
\[
y=\frac1k e^{-1}=\frac1{ke}.
\]
Therefore the maximum point is
\[
\boxed{\left(\frac1k,\frac1{ke}\right)}.
\]; At a maximum point,
\[
x=\frac1k\quad\text{and}\quad y=\frac1{ke}.
\]
Since \(1/k=x\), eliminate \(k\):
\[
y=\frac{x}{e}.
\]
Because \(k>0\), we have \(x>0\). Thus the locus is the positive ray
\[
\boxed{y=\frac{x}{e},\quad x>0}.
\] | Differentiate:
\[
y'=e^{-kx}(1-kx).
\]
Because \(e^{-kx}>0\), the derivative is positive when \(x<1/k\) and negative when \(x>1/k\). Thus the function increases and then decreases, so its absolute maximum occurs at \(x=1/k\). The maximum value is
\[
y=\frac1k e^{-1}=\frac1{ke}.
\]
Therefore the maximum point is
\[
\boxed{\left(\frac1k,\frac1{ke}\right)}.
\]; At a maximum point,
\[
x=\frac1k\quad\text{and}\quad y=\frac1{ke}.
\]
Since \(1/k=x\), eliminate \(k\):
\[
y=\frac{x}{e}.
\]
Because \(k>0\), we have \(x>0\). Thus the locus is the positive ray
\[
\boxed{y=\frac{x}{e},\quad x>0}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-06` | Revenue is \(R(x)=13x\). The profit is piecewise:
\[
P(x)=\begin{cases}
13x-(x^2+5x+7)=-x^2+8x-7,&0\le x\le3,\\
13x-[x^2+5x+7+3(x-3)]=-x^2+5x+2,&3<x\le10.
\end{cases}
\]
On \([0,3]\),
\[
P'(x)=8-2x>0,
\]
so profit increases up to \(x=3\). On \((3,10]\),
\[
P'(x)=5-2x<0,
\]
so profit decreases immediately after the threshold. The two formulas agree at the threshold, where
\[
P(3)=8.
\]
Therefore the maximizing production level is
\[
\boxed{3\text{ tons}}.
\] | Revenue is \(R(x)=13x\). The profit is piecewise:
\[
P(x)=\begin{cases}
13x-(x^2+5x+7)=-x^2+8x-7,&0\le x\le3,\\
13x-[x^2+5x+7+3(x-3)]=-x^2+5x+2,&3<x\le10.
\end{cases}
\]
On \([0,3]\),
\[
P'(x)=8-2x>0,
\]
so profit increases up to \(x=3\). On \((3,10]\),
\[
P'(x)=5-2x<0,
\]
so profit decreases immediately after the threshold. The two formulas agree at the threshold, where
\[
P(3)=8.
\]
Therefore the maximizing production level is
\[
\boxed{3\text{ tons}}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1973-07` | The line \(y=k\) meets \(y=\tan x\) at \(x=\arctan k\). Therefore
\[
A(k)=\int_0^{\arctan k}(k-\tan x)\,dx.
\]
Evaluate:
\[
A(k)=k\arctan k+\ln(\cos(\arctan k)).
\]
Since \(\cos(\arctan k)=1/\sqrt{1+k^2}\),
\[
\boxed{A(k)=k\arctan k-\frac12\ln(1+k^2)}.
\]; Substitute \(k=1\):
\[
A(1)=\arctan(1)-\frac12\ln2=\boxed{\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac12\ln2}.
\]; Differentiate the area function. The endpoint terms cancel:
\[
\frac{dA}{dk}=\arctan k.
\]
By the chain rule,
\[
\frac{dA}{dt}=\frac{dA}{dk}\frac{dk}{dt}=\arctan(k)\left(\frac1{10}\right).
\]
At \(k=1\),
\[
\boxed{\frac{dA}{dt}=\frac{\pi}{40}}
\]
square units per second. | The line \(y=k\) meets \(y=\tan x\) at \(x=\arctan k\). Therefore

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1974-02` | Symmetry about the \(y\)-axis makes \(P\) even, so the odd-power coefficients vanish:
\[
a=0,\qquad c=0.
\]
Because \((0,1)\) lies on the graph, \(d=P(0)=1\). Thus
\[
P(x)=x^4+bx^2+1.
\]
At a nonzero minimum coordinate \(q\),
\[
P'(q)=4q^3+2bq=2q(2q^2+b)=0,
\]
so \(b=-2q^2\). Also \(P(q)=-3\), so
\[
q^4+bq^2+1=-3.
\]
Substituting \(b=-2q^2\) gives \(1-q^4=-3\), hence \(q^4=4\), \(q^2=2\), and \(b=-4\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{a=0,\ b=-4,\ c=0,\ d=1},\qquad \boxed{P(x)=x^4-4x^2+1}.
\]; From the work above, \(q^4=4\), so \(q^2=2\). Both symmetric minimum points have height \(-3\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{q=\pm\sqrt2}.
\] | Symmetry about the \(y\)-axis makes \(P\) even, so the odd-power coefficients vanish:

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1974-04` | An inclination angle of \(45^\circ\) gives tangent slope
\[
f'(4)=\tan45^\circ=1.
\]
Integrate the second derivative:
\[
f'(x)=\frac23\sqrt{x+5}+C_1.
\]
Using \(f'(4)=1\),
\[
1=\frac23\sqrt9+C_1=2+C_1,
\]
so \(C_1=-1\). Integrate again:
\[
f(x)=\frac49(x+5)^{3/2}-x+C_2.
\]
Now use \(f(4)=2\):
\[
2=\frac49(9)^{3/2}-4+C_2=12-4+C_2,
\]
so \(C_2=-6\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{f(x)=\frac49(x+5)^{3/2}-x-6},\qquad x>-5.
\] | An inclination angle of \(45^\circ\) gives tangent slope

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1974-05` | Set \(y=0\). Besides \(x=0\), the positive intercept is
\[
R(m)=1000me^{-2m}.
\]
Differentiate:
\[
R'(m)=1000e^{-2m}(1-2m).
\]
Since the exponential factor is positive, \(R'(m)>0\) for \(0<m<1/2\) and \(R'(m)<0\) for \(m>1/2\). Therefore the range is maximized at
\[
\boxed{m=\frac12}.
\]; At the wall \(x=100\), the height is
\[
h(m)=100m-\frac{100^2}{1000}e^{2m}.
\]
Therefore
\[
h(m)=100m-10e^{2m}.
\]
Then
\[
h'(m)=100-20e^{2m}.
\]
Set \(h'(m)=0\):
\[
e^{2m}=5\quad\Rightarrow\quad m=\frac12\ln5.
\]
Moreover,
\[
h''(m)=-40e^{2m}<0,
\]
so this critical value gives the greatest wall height. Thus
\[
\boxed{m=\frac12\ln5}.
\] | Set \(y=0\). Besides \(x=0\), the positive intercept is

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1974-06` | Set the functions equal:
\[
\tan x=\sqrt2\cos x.
\]
Multiplying by \(\cos x>0\) gives
\[
\sin x=\sqrt2\cos^2x=\sqrt2(1-\sin^2x).
\]
The admissible solution is \(\sin x=1/\sqrt2\), so \(x=\pi/4\). Then \(y=\tan(\pi/4)=1\). Hence the intersection is
\[
\boxed{\left(\frac\pi4,1\right)}.
\]; On \([0,\pi/4]\), \(\sqrt2\cos x\) lies above \(\tan x\). Therefore
\[
A=\int_0^{\pi/4}\left(\sqrt2\cos x-\tan x\right)dx.
\]
Since \(\int-\tan x\,dx=\ln(\cos x)\),
\[
A=\left[\sqrt2\sin x+\ln(\cos x)\right]_0^{\pi/4}
=1+\ln\left(\frac1{\sqrt2}\right).
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{A=1-\frac12\ln2}.
\] | Set the functions equal:
\[

\[

\]

=1+\ln\left(\frac1{\sqrt2}\right).
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{A=1-\frac12\ln2}.
\] | verified | true |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1974-07` | Proportional growth satisfies
\[
P'(t)=kP(t),\qquad P(t)=10000e^{kt}.
\]
The two observations give
\[
10000e^{kt_1}=20000,\qquad 10000e^{k(t_1+10)}=100000.
\]
Dividing the second equation by the first yields
\[
e^{10k}=5,\qquad k=\frac{\ln5}{10}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{P(t)=10000e^{(\ln5)t/10}=10000\,5^{t/10}}.
\]; Substitute \(t=20\):
\[
P(20)=10000\,5^{20/10}=10000\cdot25=\boxed{250000}.
\]; Set \(P(t_1)=20000\):
\[
10000\,5^{t_1/10}=20000\quad\Rightarrow\quad5^{t_1/10}=2.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
\boxed{t_1=\frac{10\ln2}{\ln5}\approx4.307\text{ minutes}}.
\] | Proportional growth satisfies

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1977-02` | \[f'(x)=6x(x^2-1)^2.\]Because \((x^2-1)^2\ge0\), the derivative is positive for \(x>0\) except for the isolated stationary point \(x=1\). The function is strictly increasing across that point, so \[\boxed{f\text{ is increasing on }(0,\infty)}.\]Equivalently, a derivative sign chart may list \((0,1)\cup(1,\infty)\) and then note that the monotonic behavior continues through \(x=1\).; The derivative is negative for \(x<0\) and positive for \(x>0\), so the sign changes from negative to positive at \(x=0\). Thus \[\boxed{(0,-1)\text{ is a relative minimum}.}\]At \(x=\pm1\), the derivative is zero but does not change sign. Therefore \[\boxed{\text{there is no relative maximum}.}\]; \[f''(x)=6(x^2-1)(5x^2-1).\]This product is positive when both factors have the same sign. Therefore the graph is concave upward on \[\boxed{(-\infty,-1)\cup\left(-\frac1{\sqrt5},\frac1{\sqrt5}\right)\cup(1,\infty)}.\]; The graph is even, has an absolute/relative minimum at \((0,-1)\), crosses the x-axis with horizontal tangents at \((\pm1,0)\), has inflection points at \(x=\pm1\) and \(x=\pm1/\sqrt5\), and rises to \(+\infty\) as \(x\to\pm\infty\). | \[f'(x)=6x(x^2-1)^2.\]Because \((x^2-1)^2\ge0\), the derivative is positive for \(x>0\) except for the isolated stationary point \(x=1\). The function is strictly increasing across that point, so \[\boxed{f\text{ is increasing on }(0,\infty)}.\]Equivalently, a derivative sign chart may list \((0,1)\cup(1,\infty)\) and then note that the monotonic behavior continues through \(x=1\).; The derivative is negative for \(x<0\) and positive for \(x>0\), so the sign changes from negative to positive at \(x=0\). Thus \[\boxed{(0,-1)\text{ is a relative minimum}.}\]At \(x=\pm1\), the derivative is zero but does not change sign. Therefore \[\boxed{\text{there is no relative maximum}.}\]; \[f''(x)=6(x^2-1)(5x^2-1).\]This product is positive when both factors have the same sign. Therefore the graph is concave upward on \[\boxed{(-\infty,-1)\cup\left(-\frac1{\sqrt5},\frac1{\sqrt5}\right)\cup(1,\infty)}.\]; The graph is even, has an absolute/relative minimum at \((0,-1)\), crosses the x-axis with horizontal tangents at \((\pm1,0)\), has inflection points at \(x=\pm1\) and \(x=\pm1/\sqrt5\), and rises to \(+\infty\) as \(x\to\pm\infty\). | verified | true |

\[-3=\frac12\int_0^2(x^3+bx-2)\,dx=\frac12(4+2b-4)=b.\]
Thus
\[\boxed{a=0,\quad b=-3,\quad c=-2}.\]; The secant slope is
\[\frac{f(3)-f(0)}{3}=\frac{16-(-2)}3=6.\]
Set \(f^{\prime}(x)=3x^2-3=6\):
\[3x^2=9\Rightarrow x=\pm\sqrt3.\]
Only the value in \((0,3)\) is
\[\boxed{x=\sqrt3}.\] | verified | false |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1980-01` | The curves meet at \(x=0\) and \(x=1\). On this interval, \(\sqrt{x}\ge x^3\). Therefore
\[A=\int_0^1(\sqrt{x}-x^3)dx
=\left[\frac23x^{3/2}-\frac14x^4\right]_0^1
=\boxed{\frac5{12}}.\]; Using washers,
\[V=\pi\int_0^1\left[(\sqrt{x})^2-(x^3)^2\right]dx
=\pi\int_0^1(x-x^6)dx.\]
Thus
\[V=\pi\left[\frac{x^2}{2}-\frac{x^7}{7}\right]_0^1
=\boxed{\frac{5\pi}{14}}.\] | The curves meet at \(x=0\) and \(x=1\). On this interval, \(\sqrt{x}\ge x^3\). Therefore

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1980-02` | The rectangle has width \(2x\) and height \(4-4x^2\), so
\[A(x)=2x(4-4x^2)=8x-8x^3.\]
Then \(A^{\prime}(x)=8-24x^2\), giving \(x=1/\sqrt3\). This is a maximum because \(A^{\prime}\) changes from positive to negative. Hence
\[\boxed{C=\left(\frac1{\sqrt3},\frac83\right)}.\]; \[\frac{dA}{dt}=(8-24x^2)\frac{dx}{dt}.\]
At \(x=1/2\) and \(dx/dt=2\),
\[\frac{dA}{dt}=(8-6)(2)=\boxed{4\text{ square units per second}}.\] | The rectangle has width \(2x\) and height \(4-4x^2\), so
\[A(x)=2x(4-4x^2)=8x-8x^3.\]
Then \(A^{\prime}(x)=8-24x^2\), giving \(x=1/\sqrt3\). This is a maximum because \(A^{\prime}\) changes from positive to negative. Hence

At \(x=1/2\) and \(dx/dt=2\),
\[\frac{dA}{dt}=(8-6)(2)=\boxed{4\text{ square units per second}}.\] | verified | false |

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1980-05` | \[f(x)=\cos x(1-\cos x)=0.\]
On the interval, this gives
\[\boxed{x=-\frac\pi2,\ 0,\ \frac\pi2}.\]; \[f^{\prime}(x)=\sin x(2\cos x-1).\]
The derivative changes from positive to negative at \(x=\pm\pi/3\). Since \(f(\pm\pi/3)=1/4\), the relative maxima are
\[\boxed{\left(-\frac\pi3,\frac14\right),\quad\left(\frac\pi3,\frac14\right)}.\]; A sign chart for \(\sin x(2\cos x-1)\) gives
\[\boxed{(-\pi,-\pi/3)\cup(0,\pi/3)}.\]; The graph passes through the three intercepts, has the two relative maxima found above, is even, and reaches \(-2\) at both endpoints \(x=\pm\pi\). | \[f(x)=\cos x(1-\cos x)=0.\]

| `ECHS-APCALC-ORIGINAL-CALC-FRQ-001` | \(2(4+7)/2+3(7+9)/2+3(9+6)/2=57.5\) liters.; \(A'(5)=R(5)=9\) liters per minute; at 5 minutes, the accumulated amount is increasing at 9 liters per minute.; It is an underestimate because secant segments lie below a concave-down graph. | \(2(4+7)/2+3(7+9)/2+3(9+6)/2=57.5\) liters.; \(A'(5)=R(5)=9\) liters per minute; at 5 minutes, the accumulated amount is increasing at 9 liters per minute.; It is an underestimate because secant segments lie below a concave-down graph. | verified | true |
| `ECHS-APCALC-ORIGINAL-CALC-FRQ-002` | \(v(t)=(t-1)(t-3)\), so direction changes at \(t=1\) and \(t=3\).; \(x(5)=2+\int_0^5(t^2-4t+3)\,dt=2+\frac{20}{3}=\frac{26}{3}\).; Use absolute velocity: \(\int_0^1v(t)\,dt-\int_1^3v(t)\,dt+\int_3^5v(t)\,dt=\frac{28}{3}\) units. | \(v(t)=(t-1)(t-3)\), so direction changes at \(t=1\) and \(t=3\).; \(x(5)=2+\int_0^5(t^2-4t+3)\,dt=2+\frac{20}{3}=\frac{26}{3}\).; Use absolute velocity: \(\int_0^1v(t)\,dt-\int_1^3v(t)\,dt+\int_3^5v(t)\,dt=\frac{28}{3}\) units. | verified | true |
| `ECHS-APCALC-ORIGINAL-CALC-FRQ-003` | \(V(x)=x(20-2x)(30-2x)\), with \(0<x<10\).; \(V'=12x^2-200x+600=0\), so \(x=(25-5\sqrt7)/3\approx3.924\) cm is the only critical value in the domain and gives the maximum.; Approximately \(1056\text{ cm}^3\). | \(V(x)=x(20-2x)(30-2x)\), with \(0<x<10\).; \(V'=12x^2-200x+600=0\), so \(x=(25-5\sqrt7)/3\approx3.924\) cm is the only critical value in the domain and gives the maximum.; Approximately \(1056\text{ cm}^3\). | verified | true |

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1981-01` | Factor:
\[
f(x)=(x^2-1)(x^2-2).
\]
Thus the zeros are
\[
\boxed{x=-\sqrt2,-1,1,\sqrt2}.
\]; Since \(f(1)=0\) and
\[
f'(x)=4x^3-6x,
\]
we have \(f'(1)=-2\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{y=-2x+2}.
\]; Parallel tangents have slope \(-2\), so
\[
4x^3-6x=-2.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
2x^3-3x+1=(x-1)(2x^2+2x-1)=0.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{x=1,\quad x=\frac{-1+\sqrt3}{2},\quad x=\frac{-1-\sqrt3}{2}}.
\] | Factor:

we have \(f'(1)=-2\). Therefore

\]
Hence

\] | verified | false |

\[

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1981-04` | Because the exponent depends on \(x^2\),
\[
f(-x)=5^{\sqrt{2(-x)^2-1}}=f(x).
\]
Therefore \(\boxed{f\text{ is even}}\).; The square root requires
\[
2x^2-1\ge0.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Dom}(f)=\left(-\infty,-\frac1{\sqrt2}\right]\cup\left[\frac1{\sqrt2},\infty\right)}.
\]; The exponent \(\sqrt{2x^2-1}\) takes every value in \([0,\infty)\). Since the base is \(5\),
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Range}(f)=[1,\infty)}.
\]; At interior points of the domain,
\[
f'(x)=5^{\sqrt{2x^2-1}}\ln 5\,\frac{2x}{\sqrt{2x^2-1}}.
\]
The derivative is not finite at the two domain endpoints. | Because the exponent depends on \(x^2\),
\[
f(-x)=5^{\sqrt{2(-x)^2-1}}=f(x).
\]
Therefore \(\boxed{f\text{ is even}}\).; The square root requires
\[
2x^2-1\ge0.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Dom}(f)=\left(-\infty,-\frac1{\sqrt2}\right]\cup\left[\frac1{\sqrt2},\infty\right)}.
\]; The exponent \(\sqrt{2x^2-1}\) takes every value in \([0,\infty)\). Since the base is \(5\),
\[
\boxed{\operatorname{Range}(f)=[1,\infty)}.
\]; At interior points of the domain,
\[

\]
The derivative is not finite at the two domain endpoints. | verified | false |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1981-05` | Continuity requires
\[
5=f(2)=\lim_{x\to2^+}\left(\frac12x^2+k\right)=2+k.
\]
Hence \(\boxed{k=3}\).; For \(k=3\), the function is continuous. The left difference quotient tends to \(2\). On the right,
\[
\frac{\frac12(2+h)^2+3-5}{h}=\frac{2h+\frac12h^2}{h}=2+\frac h2\to2.
\]
Both one-sided derivatives equal \(2\), so \(\boxed{f\text{ is differentiable at }2}\).; A neighbourhood of \(x=4\) lies entirely in the polynomial branch \(\frac12x^2+4\). Therefore \(f'(4)=4\), and \(\boxed{f\text{ is differentiable at }4}\). | Continuity requires
\[
5=f(2)=\lim_{x\to2^+}\left(\frac12x^2+k\right)=2+k.
\]
Hence \(\boxed{k=3}\).; For \(k=3\), the function is continuous. The left difference quotient tends to \(2\). On the right,
\[
\frac{\frac12(2+h)^2+3-5}{h}=\frac{2h+\frac12h^2}{h}=2+\frac h2\to2.
\]
Both one-sided derivatives equal \(2\), so \(\boxed{f\text{ is differentiable at }2}\).; A neighbourhood of \(x=4\) lies entirely in the polynomial branch \(\frac12x^2+4\). Therefore \(f'(4)=4\), and \(\boxed{f\text{ is differentiable at }4}\). | verified | false |

\]; The velocity is negative on
\[
\boxed{\left(-\frac1{\sqrt2},0\right)\cup\left(\frac1{\sqrt2},1\right]}.
\] | \[

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1981-07` | \[
f_{\mathrm{avg}}=\frac1{3-1}\int_1^3f(x)\,dx=\boxed{\frac54}.
\]; First,
\[
\int_3^5f(x)\,dx=10-\frac52=\frac{15}{2}.
\]
Hence
\[
\int_3^5(2f(x)+6)\,dx=2\left(\frac{15}{2}\right)+6(2)=\boxed{27}.
\]; The two integral conditions give
\[
4a+2b=\frac52,
\qquad
12a+4b=10.
\]
Solving yields
\[
\boxed{a=\frac54,\qquad b=-\frac54}.
\] | \[
f_{\mathrm{avg}}=\frac1{3-1}\int_1^3f(x)\,dx=\boxed{\frac54}.
\]; First,
\[

\]
Hence
\[
\int_3^5(2f(x)+6)\,dx=2\left(\frac{15}{2}\right)+6(2)=\boxed{27}.
\]; The two integral conditions give
\[

\]
Solving yields

\boxed{a=\frac54,\qquad b=-\frac54}.

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-1982-02` | For \(x\ne0\),
\[
f(x)=\frac{x^2-1}{x^2-4}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=\frac14}.
\]; The numerator vanishes at \(x=0,\pm1\), but \(x=0\) is excluded from the original domain. Hence the zeros are
\[
\boxed{x=-1,1}.
\]; The reduced denominator is zero at \(x=\pm2\), so the vertical asymptotes are
\[
\boxed{x=-2\quad\text{and}\quad x=2}.
\]
The degrees and leading coefficients give the horizontal asymptote
\[
\boxed{y=1}.

| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-2007-03-FORM-B` | \[
W'(v)=-3.536v^{-0.84},
\qquad
\boxed{W'(20)\approx-0.286\ ^\circ\mathrm F/\mathrm{mph}}.
\]
At a wind velocity of \(20\) mph, the perceived wind chill decreases by approximately \(0.286^\circ\mathrm F\) for each additional mile per hour of wind speed.; \[
\frac{W(60)-W(5)}{55}\approx\boxed{-0.254\ ^\circ\mathrm F/\mathrm{mph}}.
\]
Solving \(-3.536v^{-0.84}=-0.2537967\ldots\) gives
\[
\boxed{v\approx23.011\ \mathrm{mph}}.
\]; At \(t=3\), \(v=20+5(3)=35\) mph. By the chain rule,
\[
\frac{dW}{dt}=W'(v)\frac{dv}{dt}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\frac{dW}{dt}=W'(35)(5)\approx-0.892\ ^\circ\mathrm F/\mathrm{hour}}.
\] | \[
W'(v)=-3.536v^{-0.84},
\qquad
\boxed{W'(20)\approx-0.286\ ^\circ\mathrm F/\mathrm{mph}}.
\]
At a wind velocity of \(20\) mph, the perceived wind chill decreases by approximately \(0.286^\circ\mathrm F\) for each additional mile per hour of wind speed.; \[

Then \(700+3200-2400=\boxed{1500}\), so the line is longest at \(\boxed{t=3\text{ hours}}\).; \[
\boxed{700+\int_0^t(r(x)-800)dx=0}.
\] | verified | false |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-2010-04` | Since \(x=y^2/4\),
\[
\boxed{A=\int_0^6\frac{y^2}{4}dy=18}.
\]; Using washers for \(0\le x\le9\),
\[
\boxed{V=\pi\int_0^9\left[(7-2\sqrt{x})^2-1^2\right]dx}.
\]; At height \(y\), the base length is \(y^2/4\), so the cross-sectional area is \(3y^4/16\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{V=\int_0^6\frac{3y^4}{16}dy}.
\] | Since \(x=y^2/4\),
\[
\boxed{A=\int_0^6\frac{y^2}{4}dy=18}.
\]; Using washers for \(0\le x\le9\),
\[
\boxed{V=\pi\int_0^9\left[(7-2\sqrt{x})^2-1^2\right]dx}.
\]; At height \(y\), the base length is \(y^2/4\), so the cross-sectional area is \(3y^4/16\). Therefore
\[
\boxed{V=\int_0^6\frac{3y^4}{16}dy}.
\] | verified | false |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-2010-05` | Using signed areas under \(g'\),
\[
g(3)=5+\pi+\frac32=\boxed{\frac{13}{2}+\pi},
\]
and
\[
g(-2)=5-\pi=\boxed{5-\pi}.
\]; Inflection points occur where \(g'\) changes from increasing to decreasing or conversely. These changes occur at
\[
\boxed{x=0,\ 2,\ 3}.
\]; \[
h'(x)=g'(x)-x.
\]
On the semicircle, \(\sqrt{4-x^2}=x\) gives \(x=\sqrt2\). The line segments also give \(x=3\). The derivative changes from positive to negative at \(\sqrt2\), but remains negative on both sides of \(3\). Hence
\[
\boxed{x=\sqrt2\text{ is a relative maximum};\qquad x=3\text{ is neither}}.
\] | Using signed areas under \(g'\),
\[
g(3)=5+\pi+\frac32=\boxed{\frac{13}{2}+\pi},
\]
and
\[
g(-2)=5-\pi=\boxed{5-\pi}.
\]; Inflection points occur where \(g'\) changes from increasing to decreasing or conversely. These changes occur at
\[
\boxed{x=0,\ 2,\ 3}.
\]; \[
h'(x)=g'(x)-x.
\]
On the semicircle, \(\sqrt{4-x^2}=x\) gives \(x=\sqrt2\). The line segments also give \(x=3\). The derivative changes from positive to negative at \(\sqrt2\), but remains negative on both sides of \(3\). Hence
\[
\boxed{x=\sqrt2\text{ is a relative maximum};\qquad x=3\text{ is neither}}.
\] | verified | false |
| `APCALC-AB-FRQ-2010-06` | The slope is \(f'(1)=8\). Thus
\[
\boxed{y-2=8(x-1)}.
\]; The tangent-line estimate is \(\boxed{2.8}\). Since \(f''(x)>0\) for \(1<x<1.1\), the solution is concave up and the tangent-line estimate is less than the exact value.; Separating variables and using \(f(1)=2\) gives
\[
\boxed{f(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5-4x^2}}},
\qquad -\frac{\sqrt5}{2}<x<\frac{\sqrt5}{2}.
\] | The slope is \(f'(1)=8\). Thus
\[
\boxed{y-2=8(x-1)}.
\]; The tangent-line estimate is \(\boxed{2.8}\). Since \(f''(x)>0\) for \(1<x<1.1\), the solution is concave up and the tangent-line estimate is less than the exact value.; Separating variables and using \(f(1)=2\) gives
\[
\boxed{f(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5-4x^2}}},
\qquad -\frac{\sqrt5}{2}<x<\frac{\sqrt5}{2}.
\] | verified | false |
